    *   So we cannot cover non-consecutive full triplets.
    *   Must be consecutive.

    *   **Is it possible to have 0 full triplets?**
    *   Block length 9.
    *   Triplets length 3.
    *   If no full triplet, then every triplet covered has at most 2 terms.
    *   Sum of terms covered $\le 2 \times (\text{number of triplets})$.
    *   But actually, we just need to check if such a configuration allows arbitrary $q$.
    *   If no full triplets, then for every $n$, the intersection of $[k, k+8]$ and $[3n-2, 3n]$ has size $\le 2$.
    *   Intersection size is $\max(0, \min(k+8, 3n) - \max(k, 3n-2) + 1)$.
    *   We need this $\le 2$ for all $n$.
    *   Let's check.
    *   Interval $I = [k, k+8]$.
    *   Triplets $J_n = [3n-2, 3n]$.
    *   Length of $I$ is 9.
    *   Length of $J_n$ is 3.
    *   It is impossible to shift a length 9 interval over length 3 intervals without fully covering at least one?
    *   Pigeonhole principle?
    *   Divide line into blocks of 3?
    *   Indices $1..3$ ($T_1$), $4..6$ ($T_2$), etc.
    *   Any interval of length 9 must cover at least $\lceil 9/3 \rceil = 3$ disjoint blocks? No.
    *   Consider points.
    *   If an interval of length 9 does not contain any full $[3n-2, 3n]$, then for every $n$, it misses at least 1 point of $T_n$.
    *   $T_n = \{3n-2, 3n-1, 3n\}$.
    *   If it misses $3n-2$, it must end before $3n-2$? No.
    *   If it contains subset of size $\le 2$, it must exclude at least 1 element.
    *   If it excludes $3n-2$ (first element), then $k > 3n-2 \implies k \ge 3n-1$.
    *   If it excludes $3n-1$ (middle), then either ends before $3n-1$ ($k+8 < 3n-1$) or starts after ($k > 3n-1$).
    *   If it excludes $3n$ (last), then starts after $3n$ ($k > 3n$).
    *   Let's test if we can fit $[k, k+8]$ such that it misses at least one element in every $T_n$.
    *   Actually, if it overlaps 4 triplets, say partial-partial-full-full?
    *   Wait, if length is 9, and triplets are 3.
    *   $9 = 3 \times 3$.
    *   If you shift by 1, you cover $[2, 10]$.
    *   $T_1=\{1,2,3\}$, covered $\{2,3\}$ (size 2).
    *   $T_2=\{4,5,6\}$, covered $\{4,5,6\}$ (size 3). Full.
    *   $T_3=\{7,8,9\}$, covered $\{7,8,9\}$ (size 3). Full.
    *   $T_4=\{10,11,12\}$, covered $\{10\}$ (size 1).
    *   So length 9 always contains full triplets.
    *   Is it possible to align such that you miss a full triplet?
    *   To avoid full triplet $T_n$, interval must not contain $\{3n-2, 3n-1, 3n\}$.
    *   This means either $k+8 < 3n-2$ (ends before) or $k > 3n$ (starts after) or it skips a number?
    *   But interval is contiguous.
    *   If it is contiguous and length $\ge 3$, can it avoid containing a specific set of 3 consecutive integers?
    *   Yes, if it is disjoint.
    *   But we are scanning all $n$.
    *   The union of all $T_n$ is $\mathbb{N}^*$.
    *   The interval $[k, k+8]$ is a subset of $\mathbb{N}^*$.
    *   It is a contiguous block of 9 integers.
    *   Does every contiguous block of 9 integers contain a full triplet $\{3n-2, 3n-1, 3n\}$?
    *   Let's check residues mod 3.
    *   Any block of 9 consecutive integers contains exactly 3 integers congruent to 1 mod 3, 3 to 2, 3 to 0.
    *   A triplet $T_n$ consists of residues $1, 2, 0$ (mod 3) in that order.
    *   Indices $3n-2 \equiv 1$, $3n-1 \equiv 2$, $3n \equiv 0$.
    *   So a full triplet is a specific pattern of residues $(1, 2, 0)$ starting at index $3n-2$.
    *   In a block of 9 integers, say starting at $k$.
    *   Residues depend on $k \pmod 3$.
    *   Case $k \equiv 1$: Sequence $1, 2, 0, 1, 2, 0, 1, 2, 0$.
        *   Contains $(1, 2, 0)$ at pos 1, 4, 7.
        *   Indices $k, k+1, k+2$ form $T_{(k+2)/3}$?
        *   $k=1 \implies 1,2,3$ is $T_1$.
        *   $k=4 \implies 4,5,6$ is $T_2$.
        *   $k=7 \implies 7,8,9$ is $T_3$.
        *   So 3 full triplets.
    *   Case $k \equiv 2$: Sequence $2, 0, 1, 2, 0, 1, 2, 0, 1$.
        *   Patterns $(1, 2, 0)$?
        *   Need $x_i=1, x_{i+1}=2, x_{i+2}=0$.
        *   Here we have $2, 0, 1$. No.
        *   Shifted?
        *   Wait, triplet is defined by indices, not just residues.
        *   $T_n$ is fixed set of indices.
        *   In $k \equiv 2$ case (start index 2):
        *   Indices $2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10$.
        *   $T_1=\{1,2,3\}$. Intersection $\{2,3\}$. Not full.
        *   $T_2=\{4,5,6\}$. Intersection $\{4,5,6\}$. Full.
        *   $T_3=\{7,8,9\}$. Intersection $\{7,8,9\}$. Full.
        *   $T_4=\{10,11,12\}$. Intersection $\{10\}$. Not full.
        *   So 2 full triplets.
    *   Case $k \equiv 0$ (3): Sequence $0, 1, 2, 0, 1, 2, 0, 1, 2$.
        *   Indices $3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11$.
        *   $T_1=\{1,2,3\}$. Intersection $\{3\}$.
        *   $T_2=\{4,5,6\}$. Intersection $\{4,5,6\}$. Full.
        *   $T_3=\{7,8,9\}$. Intersection $\{7,8,9\}$. Full.
        *   $T_4=\{10,11,12\}$. Intersection $\{10,11\}$.
        *   So 2 full triplets.
    *   Conclusion: Any block of 9 integers contains either 2 or 3 full triplets.
    *   If 3, contradiction.
    *   If 2, valid.
    *   The 2 full triplets are always consecutive $T_n, T_{n+1}$?
    *   In Case 2 ($k=2$), full triplets were $T_2, T_3$. Consecutive.
    *   In Case 3 ($k=3$), full triplets were $T_2, T_3$. Consecutive.
    *   In Case 1 ($k=1$), full triplets were $T_1, T_2, T_3$.
    *   So yes, whenever we have full triplets, they are consecutive.
    *   Why? Because the "gaps" between triplets are 0 (they are adjacent).
    *   $T_n = [3n-2, 3n]$, $T_{n+1} = [3n+1, 3n+3]$.
    *   Union is $[3n-2, 3n+3]$.
    *   Any interval covering parts of $T_n$ and $T_{n+2}$ must cover the gap $T_{n+1}$.
    *   So full triplets come in blocks.
    *   Since max number is 3, and 3 is impossible, we must have exactly 2 consecutive full triplets.
    *   These must be $T_n, T_{n+1}$.
    *   Constraint $q^3 = \frac{2(n+1)}{2n} = \frac{n+1}{n}$.
    *   We need to minimize $n$.
    *   We found $n=1$ impossible.
    *   $n=2$ possible.
    *   So min $n=2$.
    *   Max $q^3 = 3/2 = 1.5$.
    *   Max $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    *   **Wait, check $n$ range again.**
    *   $T_n$ indices $[3n-2, 3n]$.
    *   For $n=1$, indices $[1, 3]$.
    *   For $n=2$, indices $[4, 6]$.
    *   We established that to cover $T_1, T_2$ fully, we need $k=1$.
    *   But $k=1$ covers $T_3$ fully too.
    *   Is there any way to stop covering $T_3$?
    *   Block length 9. $T_1 \cup T_2 \cup T_3$ is exactly length 9 ($[1, 9]$).
    *   So covering $T_1, T_2$ fully requires covering $T_3$ fully?
    *   Covering $T_1$ fully $\implies k \le 1$.
    *   Covering $T_2$ fully $\implies k+8 \ge 6 \implies k \ge -2$.
    *   So $k \le 1$.
    *   If $k=1$, block is $[1, 9]$, covers $T_3$.
    *   If $k < 1$, impossible.
    *   So yes, $n=1$ is impossible.

    *   What about $n=0$?
    *   $T_0$ indices $[-2, 0]$. Not in domain.
    *   So $n \ge 1$.

    *   **Is it possible to have $q$ negative?**
    *   If $q < 0$, then $q^3 < 0$.
    *   But sum ratios are positive ($2(n+1)/2n > 0$).
    *   So $q^3$ must be positive.
    *   So $q > 0$.

    *   **Is it possible that the GP terms are not aligned with triplet boundaries in the way we assumed?**
    *   We assumed that if a triplet is fully contained, the sum of its terms is $S_{triplet} = \text{Sum of GP terms}$.
    *   This is true by definition.
    *   We assumed that for two consecutive full triplets $T_n, T_{n+1}$, the sum ratio is $q^3$.
    *   Let's verify this carefully.
    *   $T_n$ terms: $a_{3n-2}, a_{3n-1}, a_{3n}$.
    *   $T_{n+1}$ terms: $a_{3n+1}, a_{3n+2}, a_{3n+3}$.
    *   Indices of $T_{n+1}$ are indices of $T_n$ shifted by 3.
    *   $3n+1 = (3n-2) + 3$.
    *   $3n+2 = (3n-1) + 3$.
    *   $3n+3 = (3n) + 3$.
    *   Since terms are in GP with ratio $q$, $a_{j+3} = a_j q^3$.
    *   So $a_{3n+1} = a_{3n-2} q^3$, etc.
    *   Sum($T_{n+1}$) = $q^3$ Sum($T_n$).
    *   This holds regardless of where the GP starts, as long as both triplets are fully inside the GP.
    *   So the derivation is robust.

    *   **Check for any edge cases.**
    *   $a_n$ must be real.
    *   We found $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5} \approx 1.1447$.
    *   $q^3 = 1.5$.
    *   $n=2$.
    *   $k=2$ works.
    *   $a_2 = A$.
    *   $A q^2 (1+q+q^2) = 4$.
    *   $A = \frac{4}{q^2(1+q+q^2)}$.
    *   $1+q+q^2 = \frac{q^3-1}{q-1} = \frac{0.5}{q-1}$.
    *   $A = \frac{4(q-1)}{q^2(q^3-1)} = \frac{4(q-1)}{q^2(0.5)} = \frac{8(q-1)}{q^2}$.
    *   $q \approx 1.145$. $q-1 \approx 0.145$.
    *   $A \approx \frac{8(0.145)}{1.31} \approx \frac{1.16}{1.31} \approx 0.88$.
    *   $a_1 = 2 - A(1+q) \approx 2 - 0.88(2.145) \approx 2 - 1.89 \approx 0.11$.
    *   $a_{11}, a_{12}$ adjust $T_4$.
    *   Everything seems consistent.

    *   **Could $q$ be larger if we consider negative sums?**
    *   Sums are $n^2+n$, always positive for $n \ge 1$.
    *   So sums are positive.
    *   If $q$ was negative, sums would alternate signs?
    *   No, sums of triplets are fixed positive numbers.
    *   If $q < 0$, terms in a triplet might sum to a positive number.
    *   E.g. $1, -10, 11$ sum 2.
    *   But for consecutive triplets, the ratio of sums is $q^3$.
    *   If $q < 0$, $q^3 < 0$.
    *   But ratio of sums $\frac{2(n+1)}{2n} > 0$.
    *   Contradiction.
    *   So $q$ must be positive.

    *   **Could there be a case where we don't have consecutive full triplets?**
    *   We proved that covering non-consecutive full triplets implies covering the ones in between.
    *   So we always have a block of consecutive full triplets.
    *   Since max block size is 3 and 3 is impossible, block size is 2.
    *   So we always have exactly 2 consecutive full triplets $T_n, T_{n+1}$.
    *   Wait, is it possible to have block size 1?
    *   We analyzed that any block of length 9 has at least 2 full triplets.
    *   Proof:
    *   Indices $k, \dots, k+8$.
    *   $k \pmod 3$ determines alignment.
    *   If $k \equiv 1$: $1,2,3$ (full), $4,5,6$ (full), $7,8,9$ (full). 3 full.
    *   If $k \equiv 2$: $1,2,3$ (partial), $4,5,6$ (full), $7,8,9$ (full), $10,11,12$ (partial). 2 full.
    *   If $k \equiv 0$: $1,2,3$ (partial), $4,5,6$ (full), $7,8,9$ (full), $10,11,12$ (partial). 2 full.
    *   Wait, for $k \equiv 0$ (e.g. $k=3$), block is $3..11$.
    *   Triplets:
    *   $T_1 (1..3)$: intersects $\{3\}$. Partial.
    *   $T_2 (4..6)$: intersects $\{4,5,6\}$. Full.
    *   $T_3 (7..9)$: intersects $\{7,8,9\}$. Full.
    *   $T_4 (10..12)$: intersects $\{10,11\}$. Partial.
    *   So yes, exactly 2 full triplets.
    *   So min number of full triplets is 2.
    *   Max number is 3.
    *   3 is impossible.
    *   So we must have exactly 2 full triplets.
    *   These must be $T_n, T_{n+1}$.
    *   Constraint $q^3 = \frac{n+1}{n}$.
    *   We need to find possible values of $n$.
    *   For $k \equiv 1$ (Case 1), we had 3 full triplets $T_1, T_2, T_3$ (for $k=1$) or $T_m, T_{m+1}, T_{m+2}$.
    *   Generally $k=3m+1$.
    *   Triplets $T_{m+1}, T_{m+2}, T_{m+3}$ are full.
    *   Constraint $q^3 = \frac{m+2}{m+1} = \frac{m+3}{m+2}$. Impossible.
    *   So $k \equiv 1$ yields no solutions.
    *   For $k \equiv 2$ (Case 2), $k=3m+2$.
    *   Full triplets $T_{m+2}, T_{m+3}$.
    *   Indices: $T_{m+2} = [3(m+2)-2, 3(m+2)] = [3m+4, 3m+6]$.
    *   $T_{m+3} = [3m+7, 3m+9]$.
    *   Block $k..k+8 = [3m+2, 3m+10]$.
    *   Does it cover $T_{m+2}$? $3m+4 \ge 3m+2$ (Yes), $3m+6 \le 3m+10$ (Yes).
    *   Does it cover $T_{m+3}$? $3m+7 \ge 3m+2$ (Yes), $3m+9 \le 3m+10$ (Yes).
    *   Does it cover $T_{m+1}$? $T_{m+1} = [3m+1, 3m+3]$.
    *   Block starts $3m+2$. Misses $3m+1$. Not full.
    *   Does it cover $T_{m+4}$? $T_{m+4} = [3m+10, 3m+12]$.
    *   Block ends $3m+10$. Misses $3m+11, 3m+12$. Not full.
    *   So exactly 2 full triplets $T_{m+2}, T_{m+3}$.
    *   Constraint $q^3 = \frac{m+3}{m+2}$.
    *   $m$ comes from $k=3m+2$.
    *   $k \ge 1 \implies 3m+2 \ge 1 \implies 3m \ge -1 \implies m \ge 0$.
    *   Since $m$ is integer (index of triplet shift), $m \in \{0, 1, 2, \dots\}$.
    *   Wait, if $m=0$, $k=2$.
    *   Triplets $T_2, T_3$.
    *   $T_2$ sum 4, $T_3$ sum 6.
    *   $q^3 = 6/4 = 1.5$.
    *   If $m=1$, $k=5$.
    *   Triplets $T_3, T_4$.
    *   $T_3$ sum 6, $T_4$ sum 8.
    *   $q^3 = 8/6 = 4/3 \approx 1.33$.
    *   So max $q^3 = 1.5$.

    *   For $k \equiv 0$ (Case 3), $k=3m$.
    *   $k \ge 3$ (since $k \in \mathbb{N}^*$, $k=3, 6, \dots$).
    *   Wait, $k=3m$, if $m=1$, $k=3$.
    *   Triplets covered: $T_{m+1}, T_{m+2}$?
    *   Block $[3m, 3m+8]$.
    *   $T_{m+1} = [3m+1, 3m+3]$.
    *   Is it inside $[3m, 3m+8]$?
    *   $3m+1 \ge 3m$ (Yes). $3m+3 \le 3m+8$ (Yes).
    *   So $T_{m+1}$ is full.
    *   $T_{m+2} = [3m+4, 3m+6]$.
    *   Inside? Yes.
    *   $T_{m+3} = [3m+7, 3m+9]$.
    *   Ends at $3m+9 > 3m+8$. Not full.
    *   $T_m = [3m-2, 3m]$.
    *   Starts at $3m-2 < 3m$. Not full.
    *   So exactly 2 full triplets $T_{m+1}, T_{m+2}$.
    *   Constraint $q^3 = \frac{2(m+2)}{2(m+1)} = \frac{m+2}{m+1}$.
    *   $m \ge 1$.
    *   Max at $m=1 \implies q^3 = 3/2 = 1.5$.
    *   So both cases give max $q^3 = 1.5$.

    *   **Is there any trick with $n$?**
    *   The problem says "for *any* $n \in N^*$".
    *   This defines the sequence.
    *   But we only need *existence* of *some* sequence.
    *   We constructed one for $k=2$ (or $k=3$).
    *   So $q=\sqrt[3]{1.5}$ is achievable.

    *   **Check if $q$ can be larger.**
    *   We found $q^3 = \frac{m+3}{m+2}$ or $\frac{m+2}{m+1}$.
    *   These functions are decreasing for $m \ge 0$.
    *   Max value is at smallest $m$.
    *   Smallest $m=0$ for Case 2 $\implies 1.5$.
    *   Smallest $m=1$ for Case 3 $\implies 1.5$.
    *   Is it possible to have $m$ negative?
    *   $m$ is related to index $k$. $k \ge 1$.
    *   $k = 3m+2 \implies m = (k-2)/3$.
    *   If $k=2$, $m=0$.
    *   If $k=1$, $m=-1/3$ not integer.
    *   $k=3m$ implies $k$ multiple of 3. $k \ge 3 \implies m \ge 1$.
    *   So $m$ cannot be negative.
    *   So 1.5 is indeed the maximum ratio.

    *   **Are there any other constraints on $a_n$?**
    *   Maybe $a_n$ must be integers? No, "real numbers".
    *   Maybe $a_1$ must be non-zero? No.
    *   Maybe $q \ne 1$?
    *   If $q=1$, $a_n$ constant?
    *   Sum $3a_1 = 2n$? No, depends on $n$.
    *   So $q \ne 1$.
    *   But 1.5 is far from 1.

    *   **Let's double check the question wording.**
    *   "存在数列{a_n}" (Exists a sequence).
    *   "对于任意n" (For any n).
    *   "某连续9项" (Some consecutive 9 terms).
    *   "公比为q" (Common ratio q).
    *   "q的最大值" (Max value of q).

    *   Is there any interpretation where "consecutive 9 terms" means indices $n, n+1, \dots, n+8$?
    *   Yes, that's what we used ($a_k, \dots, a_{k+8}$).
    *   Wait, the problem says "某连续9项 $a_k, a_{k+1}, \cdots, a_{k+8}$".
    *   Yes.

    *   What if $q$ is negative?
    *   We argued $q^3 > 0$.
    *   What if sums were negative?
    *   $n^2+n > 0$.
    *   So sums are positive.
    *   $S_{triplet} > 0$.
    *   $q^3 = S_{next}/S_{prev} > 0$.
    *   So $q > 0$.

    *   What if $1+q+q^2 = 0$?
    *   $q$ complex. Not real.

    *   What if $a_k = 0$?
    *   If any term in GP is 0, all are 0 (if $q \ne 0$).
    *   Then sums are 0. But sums are $2n \ne 0$.
    *   So $a_k \ne 0$.
    *   So $q$ is well defined.

    *   **Is $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$ the final answer?**
    *   $1.5 = 3/2$.
    *   $\sqrt[3]{3/2}$.
    *   Can we write it as $\frac{\sqrt[3]{12}}{2}$?
    *   $\sqrt[3]{3/2} = \frac{\sqrt[3]{3}}{\sqrt[3]{2}} = \frac{\sqrt[3]{12}}{2}$.
    *   Usually $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$ or $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$ is fine.
    *   Let's check if there's any tricky interpretation.
    *   Maybe the 9 terms are $a_1, \dots, a_9$?
    *   "存在... 有某连续9项". "There exists ... a specific set of 9 consecutive terms".
    *   So we can choose the position.
    *   We chose $k=2$.
    *   This worked.

    *   **Let's re-read carefully to ensure no misinterpretation.**
    *   $a_1 + \dots + a_{3n} = n^2+n$.
    *   Wait, is it $S_{3n}$ or just sum of first $3n$?
    *   "a_1 + a_2 + \cdots + a_{3n} = n^2 + n".
    *   Yes, $S_{3n} = n^2+n$.
    *   My derivation $a_{3n-2} + a_{3n-1} + a_{3n} = 2n$ is correct.
    *   Check: $S_{3n} - S_{3(n-1)} = (n^2+n) - ((n-1)^2 + (n-1)) = n^2+n - (n^2-2n+1+n-1) = 2n$.
    *   Correct.

    *   Check $k=2$ construction again.
    *   GP terms $a_2, \dots, a_{10}$.
    *   $a_2 = A$.
    *   $a_3 = Aq$.
    *   $a_4 = Aq^2$.
    *   $a_5 = Aq^3$.
    *   $a_6 = Aq^4$.
    *   $a_7 = Aq^5$.
    *   $a_8 = Aq^6$.
    *   $a_9 = Aq^7$.
    *   $a_{10} = Aq^8$.
    *   Triplets sums:
    *   $T_1 (1,2,3)$: $a_1 + a_2 + a_3 = 2$.
        *   $a_1 + A + Aq = 2$.
        *   $a_1 = 2 - A(1+q)$.
    *   $T_2 (4,5,6)$: $a_4 + a_5 + a_6 = 4$.
        *   $Aq^2 + Aq^3 + Aq^4 = 4$.
        *   $Aq^2(1+q+q^2) = 4$.
    *   $T_3 (7,8,9)$: $a_7 + a_8 + a_9 = 6$.
        *   $Aq^5 + Aq^6 + Aq^7 = 6$.
        *   $Aq^5(1+q+q^2) = 6$.
    *   $T_4 (10,11,12)$: $a_{10} + a_{11} + a_{12} = 8$.
        *   $Aq^8 + a_{11} + a_{12} = 8$.
    *   From $T_2, T_3$:
        *   $\frac{Aq^5(1+q+q^2)}{Aq^2(1+q+q^2)} = \frac{6}{4}$.
        *   $q^3 = 1.5$.
    *   Is $1+q+q^2 \ne 0$?
        *   $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5} \approx 1.14$.
        *   $1+1.14+1.31 > 0$. Yes.
    *   Can we find $A$?
        *   $A = \frac{4}{q^2(1+q+q^2)}$.
        *   Since $q>0$, denominator $>0$, so $A$ is a well defined real number.
    *   Can we find $a_1$?
        *   $a_1 = 2 - A(1+q)$.
        *   Real number.
    *   Can we find $a_{11}, a_{12}$?
        *   Choose $a_{11} = 0, a_{12} = 8 - Aq^8$.
        *   Real numbers.
    *   We need to define the rest of the sequence?
    *   The problem only requires "existence of a sequence".
    *   We have defined $a_1, \dots, a_{12}$.
    *   What about $a_{13}, a_{14}, \dots$?
    *   We need $S_{3n} = n^2+n$ for *all* $n$.
    *   This implies $a_{3n-2} + a_{3n-1} + a_{3n} = 2n$ for all $n \ge 2$ (and $n=1$ with $S_0=0$).
    *   Actually, the condition is on sums.
    *   So we just need to ensure $a_i + a_{i+1} + a_{i+2} = 2(\text{index}/3)$ for all groups.
    *   We satisfied $T_1, T_2, T_3, T_4$ sums?
    *   $T_1$: satisfied by choice of $a_1$.
    *   $T_2$: satisfied by choice of $A$.
    *   $T_3$: satisfied by choice of $A$ (consistent).
    *   $T_4$: satisfied by choice of $a_{11}, a_{12}$.
    *   What about $T_5$?
    *   $T_5$ indices $13, 14, 15$. Sum $2(5) = 10$.
    *   We haven't defined $a_{13}, a_{14}, a_{15}$.
    *   We can choose them freely to satisfy sum 10?
    *   Wait, $a_{13}$ is part of $T_5$.
    *   Is $a_{13}$ constrained by GP?
    *   GP ends at $a_{10}$.
    *   So $a_{13}$ is free.
    *   So we can just pick $a_{13}, a_{14}, a_{15}$ such that sum is 10.
    *   And so on for all $n > 4$.
    *   So the sequence can be constructed.
    *   Thus $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$ is valid.

    *   **Is there any constraint for $n=1$?**
    *   $S_3 = 1^2+1=2$.
    *   This is $T_1$.
    *   We satisfied it.
    *   So yes.

    *   **Check if $q$ can be larger with different alignment?**
    *   We exhausted alignments $k \pmod 3$.
    *   Case 1 ($k \equiv 1$) impossible.
    *   Case 2 ($k \equiv 2$) gives $q^3 = \frac{m+3}{m+2}$. Max 1.5.
    *   Case 3 ($k \equiv 0$) gives $q^3 = \frac{m+2}{m+1}$. Max 1.5.
    *   Are there any other constraints?
    *   Maybe $a_n$ must be non-zero? Not stated.
    *   Maybe $q$ must be integer? No, "real number".

    *   **Double check calculation of $q^3$ for Case 2.**
    *   $k=3m+2$.
    *   Full triplets $T_{m+2}, T_{m+3}$.
    *   Indices:
        *   $T_{m+2}$: $3(m+2)-2 \dots 3(m+2) \implies 3m+4 \dots 3m+6$.
        *   $T_{m+3}$: $3m+7 \dots 3m+9$.
    *   Block $k \dots k+8 = 3m+2 \dots 3m+10$.
    *   Contains $3m+4, 3m+5, 3m+6$ (Full $T_{m+2}$).
    *   Contains $3m+7, 3m+8, 3m+9$ (Full $T_{m+3}$).
    *   Wait, does it contain $3m+9$?
    *   $k+8 = 3m+2+8 = 3m+10$.
    *   Yes, $3m+9 \le 3m+10$.
    *   Does it contain $3m+10$?
    *   Yes, but that's partial $T_{m+4}$.
    *   So yes, exactly two full triplets $T_{m+2}, T_{m+3}$.
    *   Sum ratio $\frac{2(m+3)}{2(m+2)} = \frac{m+3}{m+2}$.
    *   $q^3 = \frac{m+3}{m+2}$.
    *   For $m=0$, $q^3 = 3/2$.
    *   $k = 3(0)+2 = 2$.
    *   Indices $2 \dots 10$.
    *   Triplets $T_2 (4..6)$ sum 4.
    *   $T_3 (7..9)$ sum 6.
    *   Ratio $6/4 = 1.5$.
    *   Wait, if $m=0$, $T_{m+2} = T_2$, $T_{m+3} = T_3$.
    *   Is $T_2$ fully contained in $2..10$?
    *   $T_2 = \{4,5,6\}$. Yes.
    *   Is $T_3$ fully contained?
    *   $T_3 = \{7,8,9\}$. Yes.
    *   Is $T_1$ fully contained?
    *   $T_1 = \{1,2,3\}$. Intersection $\{2,3\}$. No.
    *   Is $T_4$ fully contained?
    *   $T_4 = \{10,11,12\}$. Intersection $\{10\}$. No.
    *   So yes, exactly 2.
    *   Wait, $T_2$ sum is 4?
    *   $S_6 - S_3 = (6) - (2) = 4$. Yes.
    *   $T_3$ sum is 6?
    *   $S_9 - S_6 = (12) - (6) = 6$. Yes.
    *   So $q^3 = 1.5$.

    *   **Double check calculation for Case 3.**
    *   $k=3m$.
    *   Full triplets $T_{m+1}, T_{m+2}$.
    *   Indices:
        *   $T_{m+1}$: $3m+1 \dots 3m+3$.
        *   $T_{m+2}$: $3m+4 \dots 3m+6$.
    *   Block $k \dots k+8 = 3m \dots 3m+8$.
    *   Contains $3m+1 \dots 3m+3$?
    *   $3m \le 3m+1$ (Yes). $3m+3 \le 3m+8$ (Yes).
    *   Contains $3m+4 \dots 3m+6$?
    *   $3m \le 3m+4$ (Yes). $3m+6 \le 3m+8$ (Yes).
    *   So full triplets $T_{m+1}, T_{m+2}$.
    *   Sum ratio $\frac{2(m+2)}{2(m+1)} = \frac{m+2}{m+1}$.
    *   $m \ge 1$ (since $k \ge 3$).
    *   Max at $m=1 \implies 3/2$.
    *   $k=3$.
    *   Block $3..11$.
    *   $T_2 (4..6)$ sum 4.
    *   $T_3 (7..9)$ sum 6.
    *   Ratio 1.5.
    *   Wait, if $m=1$, $T_{m+1} = T_2$.
    *   Wait, $T_2$ sum is 4.
    *   $T_3$ sum is 6.
    *   $q^3 = 6/4 = 1.5$.
    *   So both cases give 1.5.

    *   **Is it possible to have $q > \sqrt[3]{1.5}$?**
    *   Suppose $q^3 > 1.5$.
    *   Then we need $\frac{n+1}{n} > 1.5 \implies 1 + 1/n > 1.5 \implies 1/n > 0.5 \implies n < 2$.
    *   So $n=1$.
    *   This requires full triplets $T_1, T_2$.
    *   Sums 2 and 4.
    *   $q^3 = 2$.
    *   But covering $T_1, T_2$ forces covering $T_3$ as well.
    *   $T_3$ sum 6.
    *   Ratio $S_3/S_2 = 6/4 = 1.5$.
    *   But GP requires constant ratio $q^3$.
    *   So $2 = 1.5$ contradiction.
    *   So no solution for $q^3 = 2$.
    *   So $q$ cannot be larger.

    *   **Is there any loophole?**
    *   Maybe the 9 terms don't cover consecutive triplets in the way I think?
    *   But indices are consecutive integers.
    *   Triplets partition integers into chunks of 3.
    *   It's geometric.
    *   Seems solid.

    *   **Check if $q$ can be negative.**
    *   If $q < 0$, then $a_k, a_{k+1}, \dots$ alternate signs (if $q$ negative).
    *   Sum of triplet $a_{3n-2} + a_{3n-1} + a_{3n} = 2n > 0$.
    *   If $q = -r$ ($r>0$).
    *   Terms $x, -xr, xr^2, -xr^3, \dots$
    *   Sum of 3 terms $x(1 - r + r^2)$.
    *   Since $r^2 - r + 1 > 0$ for all real $r$, sum has sign of $x$.
    *   So all triplets must have same sign for first term?
    *   $a_{3n-2}$ must have sign of $2n$ (positive).
    *   So $a_{3n-2} > 0$.
    *   Then $a_{3n-1} = a_{3n-2} q < 0$.
    *   $a_{3n} = a_{3n-2} q^2 > 0$.
    *   Sum $a_{3n-2}(1+q+q^2) > 0$.
    *   This is consistent.
    *   However, ratio of sums $S_{n+1}/S_n = \frac{a_{3n+1} + a_{3n+2} + a_{3n+3}}{a_{3n-2} + a_{3n-1} + a_{3n}}$.
    *   Numerator: $a_{3n+1} + a_{3n+2} + a_{3n+3}$.
    *   Note $a_{3n+1} = a_{3n-2} q^3$.
    *   $a_{3n+2} = a_{3n-1} q^3$.
    *   $a_{3n+3} = a_{3n} q^3$.
    *   So numerator = $q^3 \times$ denominator.
    *   Ratio = $q^3$.
    *   But sum ratio is $\frac{2(n+1)}{2n} > 0$.
    *   So $q^3 > 0$.
    *   So $q > 0$.
    *   So negative $q$ is impossible.

    *   **Result:**
    *   $q_{max} = \sqrt[3]{1.5} = \sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.
    *   Can be written as $\frac{\sqrt[3]{12}}{2}$.
    *   Or $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    *   Usually in these competitions, $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$ is standard. Or maybe simplified.
    *   Let's check if there is an integer answer or simple fraction.
    *   No, cube root.

    *   **Let's check the question number.**
    *   14.
    *   Usually填空题 (fill in the blank) answers are clean.
    *   $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$ is clean enough.
    *   Maybe I missed a case where $q$ is higher?
    *   What if the GP terms are not $a_k, \dots, a_{k+8}$ but just *some* 9 terms?
    *   "某连续9项 $a_k, \dots, a_{k+8}$".
    *   Yes, consecutive.
    *   What if $n$ in $n^2+n$ is not the same $n$?
    *   "对于任意 $n \in N^*$".
    *   Yes, for all $n$.
    *   This fixes the sums of all triplets.

    *   Let's re-verify the "3 full triplets" contradiction.
    *   Triplets $T_n, T_{n+1}, T_{n+2}$.
    *   Sums $2n, 2n+2, 2n+4$.
    *   GP alignment:
    *   If $k$ aligns such that $T_n$ is full (indices $3n-2..3n$), then $a_{3n-2} = A q^p$.
    *   $T_{n+1}$ indices $3n+1..3n+3$.
    *   Shift is 3.
    *   So $T_{n+1}$ terms are $q^3$ times $T_n$ terms.
    *   Sum ratio $q^3 = \frac{2n+2}{2n} = \frac{n+1}{n}$.
    *   $T_{n+2}$ indices $3n+4..3n+6$.
    *   Shift is 3 from $T_{n+1}$.
    *   Sum ratio $q^3 = \frac{2n+4}{2n+2} = \frac{n+2}{n+1}$.
    *   Equality $\frac{n+1}{n} = \frac{n+2}{n+1} \implies (n+1)^2 = n(n+2) \implies 1=0$.
    *   Contradiction holds for any $n$.
    *   So we can never have 3 consecutive full triplets.

    *   Check if "full triplet" is the only constraint.
    *   Could we have partial triplets that force $q$ higher?
    *   Suppose block covers parts of $T_1, T_2, T_3, T_4$.
    *   E.g. $k=1$ (Case 1) covered all 3 fully.
    *   What if $k$ is not integer? No.
    *   What if block length < 9? No, fixed 9.
    *   What if $a_n$ are not defined by sums?
    *   Sums define $a_n$ partially.
    *   Actually, $a_n$ is not unique.
    *   But sums are fixed.
    *   So constraints on sums are hard constraints.
    *   The only way to avoid $q^3 = \text{ratio}$ is if the terms in $T_{n+1}$ are not $q^3$ times terms in $T_n$.
    *   But indices differ by 3.
    *   $a_{j+3} = a_j q^3$ is definition of GP.
    *   Unless terms are missing?
    *   If triplet is not full, we don't have sum relation.
    *   But we proved any block of 9 contains at least 2 full triplets.
    *   And if it contains 2 full triplets, they must be consecutive (due to geometry of intervals).
    *   And if it contains 3, contradiction.
    *   So it must contain exactly 2 consecutive full triplets.
    *   Let them be $T_n, T_{n+1}$.
    *   Then $q^3 = \frac{2(n+1)}{2n} = \frac{n+1}{n}$.
    *   We need to check if $T_n, T_{n+1}$ can exist in a block of 9 without $T_{n-1}$ or $T_{n+2}$ being full?
    *   Yes, we found examples ($k=2$ for $T_2, T_3$).
    *   For $k=2$, $T_1$ partial, $T_4$ partial.
    *   So no extra constraints.
    *   So $q^3 = \frac{n+1}{n}$ is the only constraint.
    *   To maximize $q$, minimize $n$.
    *   Smallest $n$ for which $T_n, T_{n+1}$ can be covered without $T_{n+2}$?
    *   Or without $T_{n-1}$?
    *   Actually we just need *some* $k$ such that $T_n, T_{n+1}$ are full and $T_{n+2}$ is not full (or $T_{n-1}$ not full, doesn't matter).
    *   Wait, if $T_{n+2}$ is also full, we have contradiction.
    *   So we must avoid 3 full triplets.
    *   So we need a block of 9 that covers exactly 2 full triplets (or 2 full and parts of others, but not 3 full).
    *   We found that for $k=2$, block covers $T_2, T_3$ full.
    *   Does it cover $T_4$? $T_4 = \{10, 11, 12\}$.
    *   Block ends at 10. Covers $\{10\}$. Not full.
    *   Does it cover $T_1$? $T_1 = \{1, 2, 3\}$.
    *   Block starts at 2. Covers $\{2, 3\}$. Not full.
    *   So exactly 2 full triplets.
    *   This corresponds to $n=2$ (since triplets are $T_2, T_3$).
    *   Wait, if triplets are $T_2, T_3$, then $n=2$ in formula $\frac{n+1}{n}$?
    *   Formula was derived for consecutive triplets $T_n, T_{n+1}$.
    *   Here $T_2, T_3$ are consecutive. $n=2$.
    *   Ratio $\frac{2+1}{2} = 1.5$.
    *   Is it possible to have $n=1$?
    *   Triplets $T_1, T_2$.
    *   Need block covering $\{1,2,3\}$ and $\{4,5,6\}$.
    *   Min block to cover $\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}$ is length 6.
    *   We have length 9.
    *   We can extend.
    *   To avoid covering $T_3=\{7,8,9\}$, we must end before 7?
    *   If block ends at 6, length is 6.
    *   But block length is fixed at 9.
    *   If block starts at 1, it ends at 9. Covers $T_3$.
    *   If block starts at 2, it covers $\{2..10\}$. Misses 1. So $T_1$ not full.
    *   If block starts at 0? No.
    *   So impossible to cover $T_1, T_2$ fully without covering $T_3$ or missing $T_1$.
    *   Wait, is it impossible to cover $T_1, T_2$ fully?
    *   To cover $T_1$ ($1,2,3$), block must start $\le 1$.
    *   To cover $T_2$ ($4,5,6$), block must end $\ge 6$.
    *   So block is subset of $[1, 6]$? No, contains $[1, 6]$.
    *   Block $[k, k+8]$.
    *   $k \le 1$ and $k+8 \ge 6 \implies k \ge -2$.
    *   Integer $k \ge 1$. So $k=1$.
    *   If $k=1$, block is $[1, 9]$.
    *   Contains $7,8,9$ ($T_3$).
    *   So yes, impossible to have exactly $T_1, T_2$ full.
    *   Thus $n=1$ is impossible.
    *   Next is $n=2$.
    *   Triplets $T_2 (4..6), T_3 (7..9)$.
    *   Need block covering $[4, 9]$.
    *   $k \le 4$ and $k+8 \ge 9 \implies k \ge 1$.
    *   So $k \in \{1, 2, 3, 4\}$.
    *   If $k=1$, covers $T_1$ (1..3) fully?
    *   Block $[1, 9]$. Yes. 3 full triplets.
    *   If $k=4$, block $[4, 12]$.
    *   Covers $T_4 (10..12)$ fully?
    *   $T_4$ indices 10, 11, 12.
    *   Block contains 10, 11, 12. Yes.
    *   So $k=4$ has 3 full triplets ($T_2, T_3, T_4$).
    *   If $k=2$, block $[2, 10]$.
    *   Contains $T_2, T_3$.
    *   $T_1$ (1..3): misses 1. Partial.
    *   $T_4$ (10..12): misses 11, 12. Partial.
    *   So exactly 2 full triplets.
    *   If $k=3$, block $[3, 11]$.
    *   Contains $T_2, T_3$.
    *   $T_1$ (1..3): misses 1, 2. Partial.
    *   $T_4$ (10..12): misses 12. Partial.
    *   So exactly 2 full triplets.
    *   So valid $k$ for $n=2$ are 2, 3.
    *   This gives $q^3 = 1.5$.

    *   **Is there any $n$ where we can have 2 full triplets but $n$ is smaller?**
    *   No, $n$ must be integer $\ge 1$.
    *   $n=1$ failed.
    *   So $n=2$ is minimal.
    *   So $q_{max} = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    *   **Is there any case where we don't have consecutive full triplets?**
    *   We argued no.
    *   If we have full triplets $T_a$ and $T_b$ with $a < b$.
    *   If $b > a+1$, then gap $T_{a+1}$ is fully contained in $[a, b]$?
    *   $T_a = [3a-2, 3a]$.
    *   $T_b = [3b-2, 3b]$.
    *   Union $[3a-2, 3b]$.
    *   Length $(3b) - (3a-2) + 1 = 3(b-a) + 2$.
    *   If $b=a+2$, length $3(2)+2 = 8$.
    *   Wait, length of block is 9.
    *   So block could cover $T_a$ and $T_{a+2}$ without $T_{a+1}$?
    *   Indices of $T_a$: $3a-2, 3a-1, 3a$.
    *   Indices of $T_{a+1}$: $3a+1, 3a+2, 3a+3$.
    *   Indices of $T_{a+2}$: $3a+4, 3a+5, 3a+6$.
    *   Union of $T_a, T_{a+2}$ is $\{3a-2, 3a-1, 3a, 3a+4, 3a+5, 3a+6\}$.
    *   Missing $3a+1, 3a+2, 3a+3$ ($T_{a+1}$).
    *   But block is contiguous interval.
    *   If block contains $3a$ (end of $T_a$) and $3a+4$ (start of $T_{a+2}$), it must contain everything in between.
    *   So it contains $3a+1, 3a+2, 3a+3$.
    *   So it contains $T_{a+1}$.
    *   So if block contains $T_a$ and $T_{a+2}$ fully, it must contain $T_{a+1}$ fully.
    *   So full triplets must be consecutive.
    *   This confirms we only look at consecutive pairs.

    *   **Final Verification.**
    *   Max $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    *   $1.5 = 3/2$.
    *   Is there any simplification?
    *   $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    *   Maybe write as $\frac{\sqrt[3]{12}}{2}$.
    *   Or just $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.

    *   Let's check if there is any constraint on $a_1$.
    *   $a_1 = 2 - A(1+q)$.
    *   $A = \frac{4}{q^2(1+q+q^2)}$.
    *   $a_1 = 2 - \frac{4(1+q)}{q^2(1+q+q^2)}$.
    *   With $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$, $a_1$ is a specific real number.
    *   Is there any reason $a_1$ cannot be that value?
    *   No.
    *   Is there any reason $a_{10}$ cannot be $Aq^8$?
    *   No.
    *   So the solution holds.

    *   **Alternative interpretation check.**
    *   "a_1 + ... + a_{3n} = n^2 + n".
    *   Maybe $a_n$ is defined for $n \ge 1$.
    *   Yes.
    *   Maybe $q$ must be rational? No "实数q".
    *   Maybe $q$ must be integer? No.

    *   **Could $q$ be larger if the GP is decreasing?**
    *   $q < 1$?
    *   Then $q^3 < 1$.
    *   But ratio of sums $\frac{n+1}{n} > 1$.
    *   So $q^3 > 1$.
    *   So $q > 1$.
    *   So GP is increasing.

    *   **Could the order of terms in triplet be different?**
    *   Triplet sum is commutative.
    *   $a_{3n-2} + a_{3n-1} + a_{3n}$.
    *   Order in GP is fixed by index.
    *   $a_{3n-2}$ is some term in GP.
    *   $a_{3n-1}$ is next term.
    *   $a_{3n}$ is next term.
    *   So order is preserved.

    *   **Conclusion seems robust.**
    *   Max $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    *   Usually surds are acceptable.
    *   $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$ or $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.
    *   Let's write $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.

    *   Wait, is it possible to have $n$ very large?
    *   $q^3 = \frac{n+1}{n} \to 1$.
    *   $q \to 1$.
    *   We want max $q$.
    *   So small $n$.

    *   Double check $n=1$ impossibility again.
    *   We need $T_1, T_2$ full.
    *   $T_1=\{1,2,3\}, T_2=\{4,5,6\}$.
    *   Block $[k, k+8]$.
    *   Must contain $\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}$.

    *   Contradiction.
    *   Correct.

    *   Wait, is it possible that $a_7, a_8, a_9$ are not in GP?
    *   "有某连续9项 ... 是公比为q的等比数列".
    *   The block is $a_k, \dots, a_{k+8}$.
    *   If $k=1$, block is $a_1, \dots, a_9$.
    *   These 9 terms are in GP.
    *   $T_1$ terms are $a_1, a_2, a_3$.
    *   $T_2$ terms are $a_4, a_5, a_6$.
    *   $T_3$ terms are $a_7, a_8, a_9$.
    *   Since block is GP, $a_4 = a_1 q^3$, etc.
    *   So $S_2 = q^3 S_1$ and $S_3 = q^3 S_2$.
    *   $4 = q^3(2) \implies q^3=2$.
    *   $6 = q^3(4) \implies q^3=1.5$.
    *   Contradiction.
    *   Correct.

    *   What if $k=2$?
    *   Block $a_2, \dots, a_{10}$.
    *   $T_1$ terms $a_1, a_2, a_3$.
    *   GP contains $a_2, a_3$.
    *   $a_1$ is not in GP.
    *   So we cannot relate $S_1$ to GP ratio directly.
    *   $T_2$ terms $a_4, a_5, a_6$. All in GP.
    *   $T_3$ terms $a_7, a_8, a_9$. All in GP.
    *   $T_4$ terms $a_{10}, a_{11}, a_{12}$.
    *   GP contains $a_{10}$.
    *   Relation between $T_2$ and $T_3$:
    *   $a_7 = a_4 q^3$, etc.
    *   So $S_3 = q^3 S_2$.
    *   $6 = q^3(4) \implies q^3 = 1.5$.
    *   Relation with $T_1$?
    *   $S_1 = a_1 + a_2 + a_3 = 2$.
    *   $a_2, a_3$ in GP. $a_2=A, a_3=Aq$.
    *   $a_1 + A(1+q) = 2$.
    *   $a_1$ is free.
    *   No constraint on $q$ from $T_1$.
    *   Relation with $T_4$?
    *   $S_4 = a_{10} + a_{11} + a_{12} = 8$.
    *   $a_{10} = Aq^8$.
    *   $a_{11}, a_{12}$ free.
    *   No constraint.
    *   So $q^3 = 1.5$ is valid.

    *   So logic holds.

    *   I'll provide $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$ or equivalent.

    *   Wait, is $q$ required to be positive?
    *   If $q$ is negative, $q^3$ is negative.
    *   But ratio of sums is positive.
    *   So $q$ must be positive.

    *   Is there any constraint on $a_n$ being non-zero?
    *   If $a_k=0$, then $q$ undefined or all 0.
    *   If all 0, sums 0. Contradiction.
    *   So $a_k \ne 0$.
    *   $q$ defined.

    *   One check: $n^2+n$.
    *   $n=1 \implies 2$.
    *   $n=2 \implies 6$.
    *   $n=3 \implies 12$.
    *   Triplets sums: 2, 4, 6.
    *   Wait, $S_{3n} = n^2+n$.
    *   $S_3 = 2$. $a_1+a_2+a_3 = 2$.
    *   $S_6 = 6$. $a_1+\dots+a_6 = 6$.
    *   $a_4+a_5+a_6 = 6-2 = 4$.
    *   $S_9 = 12$. $a_1+\dots+a_9 = 12$.
    *   $a_7+a_8+a_9 = 12-6 = 6$.
    *   Triplets sums: 2, 4, 6.
    *   Wait, earlier I said $T_n$ sum is $2n$.
    *   $T_1$ sum 2.
    *   $T_2$ sum 4.
    *   $T_3$ sum 6.
    *   Yes.
    *   My derivation used $T_n$ sum $2n$.
    *   Consistent.

    *   Wait, in $k=2$ case ($T_2, T_3$), sums are 4 and 6.
    *   Ratio $6/4 = 1.5$.
    *   In $k=3$ case ($T_2, T_3$), sums are 4 and 6.
    *   Ratio 1.5.
    *   Wait, if $k=3$, block $3..11$.
    *   $T_2 (4..6)$ sum 4.
    *   $T_3 (7..9)$ sum 6.
    *   $T_1 (1..3)$ sum 2.
    *   $T_4 (10..12)$ sum 8.
    *   Block contains $T_2, T_3$ fully.
    *   $a_3$ is in $T_1$ (partial) and start of GP.
    *   $a_{10}, a_{11}$ in $T_4$ (partial) and end of GP.
    *   Relations:
    *   $T_2$ sum 4.
    *   $T_3$ sum 6.
    *   $S_3/S_2 = 6/4 = 1.5$.
    *   $q^3 = 1.5$.
    *   So $q$ same.

    *   What about $k=5$? (Case 2, $m=1$).
    *   Block $5..13$.
    *   Full triplets $T_3 (7..9)$ sum 6, $T_4 (10..12)$ sum 8.
    *   Ratio $8/6 = 4/3 \approx 1.33$.
    *   $q = \sqrt[3]{1.33}$.
    *   Smaller.

    *   What about $k=6$? (Case 3, $m=2$).
    *   Block $6..14$.
    *   Full triplets $T_3 (7..9)$ sum 6, $T_4 (10..12)$ sum 8.
    *   Ratio $4/3$.

    *   So max is indeed at smallest valid block position covering smallest sums with ratio > 1.
    *   Smallest sums are 2, 4, 6.
    *   We can't use 2 and 4 because that requires covering $T_1, T_2$, which forces covering $T_3$ (sum 6), creating contradiction.
    *   So we must skip $T_1$ or stop before $T_3$?
    *   Can't stop before $T_3$ if we cover $T_2$ (since length 9 is long).
    *   So we must skip $T_1$ (start after 1).
    *   Start at 2 ($k=2$).
    *   Cover $T_2, T_3$.
    *   Sums 4, 6.
    *   Ratio 1.5.
    *   Valid.

    *   Start at 3 ($k=3$).
    *   Cover $T_2, T_3$.
    *   Same.

    *   So 1.5 is max.

    *   Final check on value.
    *   $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    *   Decimal $\approx 1.1447$.
    *   Is it possible to have $q=2$?
    *   $q^3=8$.
    *   Ratio 8.
    *   Sums $x, 8x$.
    *   Sums are $2n, 2n+2$.
    *   $2n+2 = 8(2n) = 16n \implies 2 = 14n \implies n=1/7$.
    *   Not integer.
    *   So no.

    *   So answer is $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.
